My wife actually pushed me to come in because I'm falling so far behind with my clients and can't seem to manage basic tasks anymore.\n\nDoctor: During the wreck, did you hit your head or lose consciousness, and have you been experiencing physical symptoms like headaches or dizziness since then?\n\nPatient: To be honest, I don't recall hitting my head because there is a big gap in my memory right after the impact until I was suddenly outside on the stretcher. I don't think I've been having any headaches or dizziness since then, though.\n\nDoctor: Since the wreck, have you been experiencing any nightmares, sudden flashbacks, or a strong urge to avoid driving or thinking about the accident?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had any nightmares or sudden flashbacks, and I haven't been trying to avoid driving or thinking about it. But when I do ride in a car, I feel very detached, almost like everything is fake and I'm just watching a movie of myself instead of actually being there.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you noticed any other changes like feeling unusually fatigued, having trouble sleeping, or feeling more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed feeling unusually tired or having trouble sleeping since the accident. I don't think I've been particularly irritable either.\n\nDoctor: Were you evaluated at a hospital or clinic after the accident, and did the doctors mention if you had sustained a concussion or head injury?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was taken to the ER and evaluated right after the accident that night. They discharged me a few hours later with just some minor bumps and bruises, and the doctors never mentioned anything about a concussion or head injury.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with thinking and decision-making present all the time, or do they mainly happen when you are feeling detached and like things are unreal?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure if they are connected. The thinking trouble seems to happen mostly when I'm sitting at my desk trying to do my work, while that detached feeling usually hits me when I'm riding in a car or near the intersection. I haven't really tracked if they occur at the exact same time.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you noticed any other cognitive issues, such as forgetting recent conversations, misplacing items, or having trouble remembering appointments?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed misplacing things or forgetting conversations, but my ability to manage my schedule and appointments is severely impaired. I've missed multiple scheduled calls, and my wife has had to take over organizing my appointments and paying bills because I just can't think clearly enough to keep track of it all.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of being detached or like things are unreal happen at other times, such as when you are at home or work, or does it only happen when you are in a car or near the accident scene?\n\nPatient: It really only happens in those specific moments, like when I'm riding in a car or walking past the intersection where the crash happened. Everything suddenly feels weirdly far away and fake, like I'm watching a movie of my own life. It feels like I'm not even inside my own body, but just sort of floating a few feet behind myself.\n\nDoctor: Before this accident occurred two weeks ago, had you ever experienced similar episodes of feeling detached from yourself, or had any of these difficulties with concentration and planning?\n\nPatient: No, I have never experienced anything like this before in my life. Before the wreck two weeks ago, I was highly functional, ran my own tax business, and managed my daily responsibilities with absolutely no issues. All of these concentration problems and that detached feeling started strictly after the accident.\n\nDoctor: Did the doctors at the emergency room perform any head imaging, like a CT scan, and have you noticed any sensitivity to light or loud noises since the accident?\n\nPatient: I don't think they did a CT scan at the ER, since they just checked me over pretty quickly and discharged me with minor injuries. I haven't really noticed any sensitivity to light, and while loud noises don't necessarily hurt my ears, sudden noises do make me jump right out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: Even though you haven't had full flashbacks, do you find yourself having sudden, unwanted memories or images of the accident popping into your mind during the day?"
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don't really experience those sudden, unwanted images of the accident popping into my head during the day. Aside from that gap in my memory, I don't have pictures or memories of the wreck randomly showing up.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Ever since the wreck two weeks ago, I just can’t think straight long enough to do my work or make simple decisions, it’s like my mind keeps stalling out. I don't think I've been having any headaches or dizziness since then, though.\n\nDoctor: Since the wreck, have you been experiencing any nightmares, sudden flashbacks, or a strong urge to avoid driving or thinking about the accident?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had any nightmares or sudden flashbacks, and I haven't been trying to avoid driving or thinking about it. But when I do ride in a car, I feel very detached, almost like everything is fake and I'm just watching a movie of myself instead of actually being there.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you noticed any other changes like feeling unusually fatigued, having trouble sleeping, or feeling more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed feeling unusually tired or having trouble sleeping since the accident. I don't think I've been particularly irritable either.\n\nDoctor: Were you evaluated at a hospital or clinic after the accident, and did the doctors mention if you had sustained a concussion or head injury?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was taken to the ER and evaluated right after the accident that night. They discharged me a few hours later with just some minor bumps and bruises, and the doctors never mentioned anything about a concussion or head injury.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with thinking and decision-making present all the time, or do they mainly happen when you are feeling detached and like things are unreal?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure if they are connected. I've missed multiple scheduled calls, and my wife has had to take over organizing my appointments and paying bills because I just can't think clearly enough to keep track of it all.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of being detached or like things are unreal happen at other times, such as when you are at home or work, or does it only happen when you are in a car or near the accident scene?\n\nPatient: It really only happens in those specific moments, like when I'm riding in a car or walking past the intersection where the crash happened. Everything suddenly feels weirdly far away and fake, like I'm watching a movie of my own life. It feels like I'm not even inside my own body, but just sort of floating a few feet behind myself.\n\nDoctor: Before this accident occurred two weeks ago, had you ever experienced similar episodes of feeling detached from yourself, or had any of these difficulties with concentration and planning?\n\nPatient: No, I have never experienced anything like this before in my life. I haven't really noticed any sensitivity to light, and while loud noises don't necessarily hurt my ears, sudden noises do make me jump right out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: Even though you haven't had full flashbacks, do you find yourself having sudden, unwanted memories or images of the accident popping into your mind during the day?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience those sudden, unwanted images of the accident popping into my head during the day. Aside from that gap in my memory, I don't have pictures or memories of the wreck randomly showing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you know if you actually lost consciousness during that gap in your memory, and if so, for about how long?\n\nPatient: I don't think I actually lost consciousness, at least according to what the paramedics and hospital staff told me later, but I honestly don't know for sure because of that massive blank spot. There is just a complete, frustrating gap in my mind between seeing those headlights coming at me and suddenly being outside on the stretcher.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the moments when you are in a car, how has your general mood been since the accident? Have you been feeling particularly anxious, down, or hopeless?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I feel sad, but my mood is completely flat and disconnected, to the point where I can't even feel happy or grateful when my wife or son shares good news. At the same time, I feel constantly anxious and on edge, always scanning my surroundings for danger even when I'm just sitting in my own living room. But when I do ride in a car, I feel very detached, almost like everything is fake and I'm just watching a movie of myself instead of actually being there.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you noticed any other changes like feeling unusually fatigued, having trouble sleeping, or feeling more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed feeling unusually tired or having trouble sleeping since the accident. I don't think I've been particularly irritable either.\n\nDoctor: Were you evaluated at a hospital or clinic after the accident, and did the doctors mention if you had sustained a concussion or head injury?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was taken to the ER and evaluated right after the accident that night. They discharged me a few hours later with just some minor bumps and bruises, and the doctors never mentioned anything about a concussion or head injury.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with thinking and decision-making present all the time, or do they mainly happen when you are feeling detached and like things are unreal?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure if they are connected. The thinking trouble seems to happen mostly when I'm sitting at my desk trying to do my work, while that detached feeling usually hits me when I'm riding in a car or near the intersection. I haven't really tracked if they occur at the exact same time.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you noticed any other cognitive issues, such as forgetting recent conversations, misplacing items, or having trouble remembering appointments?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed misplacing things or forgetting conversations, but my ability to manage my schedule and appointments is severely impaired. I've missed multiple scheduled calls, and my wife has had to take over organizing my appointments and paying bills because I just can't think clearly enough to keep track of it all.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of being detached or like things are unreal happen at other times, such as when you are at home or work, or does it only happen when you are in a car or near the accident scene?\n\nPatient: It really only happens in those specific moments, like when I'm riding in a car or walking past the intersection where the crash happened. Everything suddenly feels weirdly far away and fake, like I'm watching a movie of my own life. It feels like I'm not even inside my own body, but just sort of floating a few feet behind myself.\n\nDoctor: Before this accident occurred two weeks ago, had you ever experienced similar episodes of feeling detached from yourself, or had any of these difficulties with concentration and planning?\n\nPatient: No, I have never experienced anything like this before in my life. Before the wreck two weeks ago, I was highly functional, ran my own tax business, and managed my daily responsibilities with absolutely no issues. All of these concentration problems and that detached feeling started strictly after the accident.\n\nDoctor: Did the doctors at the emergency room perform any head imaging, like a CT scan, and have you noticed any sensitivity to light or loud noises since the accident?\n\nPatient: I don't think they did a CT scan at the ER, since they just checked me over pretty quickly and discharged me with minor injuries. I haven't really noticed any sensitivity to light, and while loud noises don't necessarily hurt my ears, sudden noises do make me jump right out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: Even though you haven't had full flashbacks, do you find yourself having sudden, unwanted memories or images of the accident popping into your mind during the day?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience those sudden, unwanted images of the accident popping into my head during the day. Aside from that gap in my memory, I don't have pictures or memories of the wreck randomly showing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you know if you actually lost consciousness during that gap in your memory, and if so, for about how long?\n\nPatient: I don't think I actually lost consciousness, at least according to what the paramedics and hospital staff told me later, but I honestly don't know for sure because of that massive blank spot. There is just a complete, frustrating gap in my mind between seeing those headlights coming at me and suddenly being outside on the stretcher.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the moments when you are in a car, how has your general mood been since the accident? Have you been feeling particularly anxious, down, or hopeless?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I feel sad, but my mood is completely flat and disconnected, to the point where I can't even feel happy or grateful when my wife or son shares good news. At the same time, I feel constantly anxious and on edge, always scanning my surroundings for danger even when I'm just sitting in my own living room. It's like I'm always waiting for something bad to happen again.\n\nDoctor: When you are in a car or near the intersection where the accident happened, do you experience physical reactions like a racing heart, sweating, or difficulty breathing?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience those specific physical reactions like a racing heart, sweating, or trouble breathing when I'm in those situations. For me, it is really just that weird, detached feeling where everything around me suddenly feels fake."
  }

My wife actually pushed me to come in because I'm falling so far behind with my clients and can't seem to manage basic tasks anymore.\n\nDoctor: During the wreck, did you hit your head or lose consciousness, and have you been experiencing physical symptoms like headaches or dizziness since then?\n\nPatient: To be honest, I don't recall hitting my head because there is a big gap in my memory right after the impact until I was suddenly outside on the stretcher. I don't think I've been having any headaches or dizziness since then, though.\n\nDoctor: Since the wreck, have you been experiencing any nightmares, sudden flashbacks, or a strong urge to avoid driving or thinking about the accident?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had any nightmares or sudden flashbacks, and I haven't been trying to avoid driving or thinking about it. But when I do ride in a car, I feel very detached, almost like everything is fake and I'm just watching a movie of myself instead of actually being there.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you noticed any other changes like feeling unusually fatigued, having trouble sleeping, or feeling more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed feeling unusually tired or having trouble sleeping since the accident. I don't think I've been particularly irritable either.\n\nDoctor: Were you evaluated at a hospital or clinic after the accident, and did the doctors mention if you had sustained a concussion or head injury?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was taken to the ER and evaluated right after the accident that night. They discharged me a few hours later with just some minor bumps and bruises, and the doctors never mentioned anything about a concussion or head injury.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with thinking and decision-making present all the time, or do they mainly happen when you are feeling detached and like things are unreal?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure if they are connected. The thinking trouble seems to happen mostly when I'm sitting at my desk trying to do my work, while that detached feeling usually hits me when I'm riding in a car or near the intersection. I haven't really tracked if they occur at the exact same time.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you noticed any other cognitive issues, such as forgetting recent conversations, misplacing items, or having trouble remembering appointments?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed misplacing things or forgetting conversations, but my ability to manage my schedule and appointments is severely impaired. I've missed multiple scheduled calls, and my wife has had to take over organizing my appointments and paying bills because I just can't think clearly enough to keep track of it all.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of being detached or like things are unreal happen at other times, such as when you are at home or work, or does it only happen when you are in a car or near the accident scene?\n\nPatient: It really only happens in those specific moments, like when I'm riding in a car or walking past the intersection where the crash happened. Everything suddenly feels weirdly far away and fake, like I'm watching a movie of my own life. It feels like I'm not even inside my own body, but just sort of floating a few feet behind myself.\n\nDoctor: Before this accident occurred two weeks ago, had you ever experienced similar episodes of feeling detached from yourself, or had any of these difficulties with concentration and planning?\n\nPatient: No, I have never experienced anything like this before in my life. Before the wreck two weeks ago, I was highly functional, ran my own tax business, and managed my daily responsibilities with absolutely no issues. All of these concentration problems and that detached feeling started strictly after the accident.\n\nDoctor: Did the doctors at the emergency room perform any head imaging, like a CT scan, and have you noticed any sensitivity to light or loud noises since the accident?\n\nPatient: I don't think they did a CT scan at the ER, since they just checked me over pretty quickly and discharged me with minor injuries. I haven't really noticed any sensitivity to light, and while loud noises don't necessarily hurt my ears, sudden noises do make me jump right out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: Even though you haven't had full flashbacks, do you find yourself having sudden, unwanted memories or images of the accident popping into your mind during the day?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience those sudden, unwanted images of the accident popping into my head during the day. Aside from that gap in my memory, I don't have pictures or memories of the wreck randomly showing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you know if you actually lost consciousness during that gap in your memory, and if so, for about how long?\n\nPatient: I don't think I actually lost consciousness, at least according to what the paramedics and hospital staff told me later, but I honestly don't know for sure because of that massive blank spot. There is just a complete, frustrating gap in my mind between seeing those headlights coming at me and suddenly being outside on the stretcher.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the moments when you are in a car, how has your general mood been since the accident? Have you been feeling particularly anxious, down, or hopeless?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I feel sad, but my mood is completely flat and disconnected, to the point where I can't even feel happy or grateful when my wife or son shares good news. At the same time, I feel constantly anxious and on edge, always scanning my surroundings for danger even when I'm just sitting in my own living room. It's like I'm always waiting for something bad to happen again.\n\nDoctor: When you are in a car or near the intersection where the accident happened, do you experience physical reactions like a racing heart, sweating, or difficulty breathing?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience those specific physical reactions like a racing heart, sweating, or trouble breathing when I'm in those situations. For me, it is really just that weird, detached feeling where everything around me suddenly feels fake.\n\nDoctor: Apart from the gap during the accident itself, have you noticed any other gaps in your memory, such as forgetting important personal information or other events that happened before or after that day?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't forgotten any personal information or other events before or after that day. The memory gap is strictly limited to those few minutes of the accident itself, between seeing the headlights and being on the stretcher."

I don't think I've been having any headaches or dizziness since then, though.\n\nDoctor: Since the wreck, have you been experiencing any nightmares, sudden flashbacks, or a strong urge to avoid driving or thinking about the accident?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had any nightmares or sudden flashbacks, and I haven't been trying to avoid driving or thinking about it. But when I do ride in a car, I feel very detached, almost like everything is fake and I'm just watching a movie of myself instead of actually being there.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you noticed any other changes like feeling unusually fatigued, having trouble sleeping, or feeling more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed feeling unusually tired or having trouble sleeping since the accident. I don't think I've been particularly irritable either.\n\nDoctor: Were you evaluated at a hospital or clinic after the accident, and did the doctors mention if you had sustained a concussion or head injury?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was taken to the ER and evaluated right after the accident that night. They discharged me a few hours later with just some minor bumps and bruises, and the doctors never mentioned anything about a concussion or head injury.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with thinking and decision-making present all the time, or do they mainly happen when you are feeling detached and like things are unreal?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure if they are connected. The thinking trouble seems to happen mostly when I'm sitting at my desk trying to do my work, while that detached feeling usually hits me when I'm riding in a car or near the intersection. I haven't really tracked if they occur at the exact same time.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you noticed any other cognitive issues, such as forgetting recent conversations, misplacing items, or having trouble remembering appointments?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed misplacing things or forgetting conversations, but my ability to manage my schedule and appointments is severely impaired. I've missed multiple scheduled calls, and my wife has had to take over organizing my appointments and paying bills because I just can't think clearly enough to keep track of it all.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of being detached or like things are unreal happen at other times, such as when you are at home or work, or does it only happen when you are in a car or near the accident scene?\n\nPatient: It really only happens in those specific moments, like when I'm riding in a car or walking past the intersection where the crash happened. Everything suddenly feels weirdly far away and fake, like I'm watching a movie of my own life. I haven't really noticed any sensitivity to light, and while loud noises don't necessarily hurt my ears, sudden noises do make me jump right out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: Even though you haven't had full flashbacks, do you find yourself having sudden, unwanted memories or images of the accident popping into your mind during the day?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience those sudden, unwanted images of the accident popping into my head during the day. Aside from that gap in my memory, I don't have pictures or memories of the wreck randomly showing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you know if you actually lost consciousness during that gap in your memory, and if so, for about how long?\n\nPatient: I don't think I actually lost consciousness, at least according to what the paramedics and hospital staff told me later, but I honestly don't know for sure because of that massive blank spot. There is just a complete, frustrating gap in my mind between seeing those headlights coming at me and suddenly being outside on the stretcher.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the moments when you are in a car, how has your general mood been since the accident? At the same time, I feel constantly anxious and on edge, always scanning my surroundings for danger even when I'm just sitting in my own living room. It's like I'm always waiting for something bad to happen again.\n\nDoctor: When you are in a car or near the intersection where the accident happened, do you experience physical reactions like a racing heart, sweating, or difficulty breathing?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience those specific physical reactions like a racing heart, sweating, or trouble breathing when I'm in those situations. For me, it is really just that weird, detached feeling where everything around me suddenly feels fake.\n\nDoctor: Apart from the gap during the accident itself, have you noticed any other gaps in your memory, such as forgetting important personal information or other events that happened before or after that day?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't forgotten any personal information or other events before or after that day. The memory gap is strictly limited to those few minutes of the accident itself, between seeing the headlights and being on the stretcher.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you noticed any new, strong negative thoughts about yourself or the world, such as feeling like you can't trust anyone or that nowhere is safe?\n\nPatient: I haven't really had those specific thoughts about not trusting people or the world being completely unsafe. It's more that I just feel constantly on edge and worried that something bad is about to happen again."

The memory gap is strictly limited to those few minutes of the accident itself, between seeing the headlights and being on the stretcher.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you noticed any new, strong negative thoughts about yourself or the world, such as feeling like you can't trust anyone or that nowhere is safe?\n\nPatient: I haven't really had those specific thoughts about not trusting people or the world being completely unsafe. I've missed multiple scheduled calls, and my wife has had to take over organizing my appointments and paying bills because I just can't think clearly enough to keep track of it all.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of being detached or like things are unreal happen at other times, such as when you are at home or work, or does it only happen when you are in a car or near the accident scene?\n\nPatient: It really only happens in those specific moments, like when I'm riding in a car or walking past the intersection where the crash happened. Everything suddenly feels weirdly far away and fake, like I'm watching a movie of my own life. It feels like I'm not even inside my own body, but just sort of floating a few feet behind myself.\n\nDoctor: Before this accident occurred two weeks ago, had you ever experienced similar episodes of feeling detached from yourself, or had any of these difficulties with concentration and planning?\n\nPatient: No, I have never experienced anything like this before in my life. Before the wreck two weeks ago, I was highly functional, ran my own tax business, and managed my daily responsibilities with absolutely no issues. All of these concentration problems and that detached feeling started strictly after the accident.\n\nDoctor: Did the doctors at the emergency room perform any head imaging, like a CT scan, and have you noticed any sensitivity to light or loud noises since the accident?\n\nPatient: I don't think they did a CT scan at the ER, since they just checked me over pretty quickly and discharged me with minor injuries. I haven't really noticed any sensitivity to light, and while loud noises don't necessarily hurt my ears, sudden noises do make me jump right out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: Even though you haven't had full flashbacks, do you find yourself having sudden, unwanted memories or images of the accident popping into your mind during the day?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience those sudden, unwanted images of the accident popping into my head during the day. Aside from that gap in my memory, I don't have pictures or memories of the wreck randomly showing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you know if you actually lost consciousness during that gap in your memory, and if so, for about how long?\n\nPatient: I don't think I actually lost consciousness, at least according to what the paramedics and hospital staff told me later, but I honestly don't know for sure because of that massive blank spot. There is just a complete, frustrating gap in my mind between seeing those headlights coming at me and suddenly being outside on the stretcher.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the moments when you are in a car, how has your general mood been since the accident? Have you been feeling particularly anxious, down, or hopeless?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I feel sad, but my mood is completely flat and disconnected, to the point where I can't even feel happy or grateful when my wife or son shares good news. At the same time, I feel constantly anxious and on edge, always scanning my surroundings for danger even when I'm just sitting in my own living room. It's like I'm always waiting for something bad to happen again.\n\nDoctor: When you are in a car or near the intersection where the accident happened, do you experience physical reactions like a racing heart, sweating, or difficulty breathing?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience those specific physical reactions like a racing heart, sweating, or trouble breathing when I'm in those situations. For me, it is really just that weird, detached feeling where everything around me suddenly feels fake.\n\nDoctor: Apart from the gap during the accident itself, have you noticed any other gaps in your memory, such as forgetting important personal information or other events that happened before or after that day?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't forgotten any personal information or other events before or after that day. The memory gap is strictly limited to those few minutes of the accident itself, between seeing the headlights and being on the stretcher.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you noticed any new, strong negative thoughts about yourself or the world, such as feeling like you can't trust anyone or that nowhere is safe?\n\nPatient: I haven't really had those specific thoughts about not trusting people or the world being completely unsafe. It's more that I just feel constantly on edge and worried that something bad is about to happen again.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned feeling detached when riding in a car. Have you actually driven a car yourself since the accident, or have you been avoiding getting behind the wheel?\n\nPatient: I haven't actually tried to drive myself since the accident happened. My wife has been driving me everywhere because I just haven't felt up to getting behind the wheel.\n\nDoctor: When you think about driving or passing that intersection, do you actively avoid doing so because you want to prevent that detached feeling or anxiety from happening?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I haven't really thought about actively trying to avoid those situations. It's just that when I do find myself in a car or near that intersection, that strange, detached feeling always hits me.

weeks ago and has been going on constantly since then.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: “Ever since the wreck I just can’t think straight long enough to do my work or make simple decisions, it’s like my mind keeps stalling out.”\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. Do not list your symptoms unprompted.\n- Speak naturally and briefly, as a real patient would (2–3 sentences max).\n- Do not mention symptoms that the doctor has not asked about.\n- If asked about a symptom not in your profile, say you are not sure.\n- Respond in English.\n\n\n[This Turn: Profile Alignment Result — Follow Strictly]\n(No match in profile or inference only)\nAnswer strategy: State that you haven't really had nightmares or found yourself constantly replaying the accident in your mind, keeping the response brief and direct."

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Ever since the wreck two weeks ago, I just can’t think straight long enough to do my work or make simple decisions, it’s like my mind keeps stalling out. My wife actually pushed me to come in because I'm falling so far behind with my clients and can't seem to manage basic tasks anymore.\n\nDoctor: During the wreck, did you hit your head or lose consciousness, and have you been experiencing physical symptoms like headaches or dizziness since then?\n\nPatient: To be honest, I don't recall hitting my head because there is a big gap in my memory right after the impact until I was suddenly outside on the stretcher. I don't think I've been having any headaches or dizziness since then, though.\n\nDoctor: Since the wreck, have you been experiencing any nightmares, sudden flashbacks, or a strong urge to avoid driving or thinking about the accident?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had any nightmares or sudden flashbacks, and I haven't been trying to avoid driving or thinking about it. But when I do ride in a car, I feel very detached, almost like everything is fake and I'm just watching a movie of myself instead of actually being there.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you noticed any other changes like feeling unusually fatigued, having trouble sleeping, or feeling more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed feeling unusually tired or having trouble sleeping since the accident. I don't think I've been particularly irritable either.\n\nDoctor: Were you evaluated at a hospital or clinic after the accident, and did the doctors mention if you had sustained a concussion or head injury?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was taken to the ER and evaluated right after the accident that night. They discharged me a few hours later with just some minor bumps and bruises, and the doctors never mentioned anything about a concussion or head injury.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with thinking and decision-making present all the time, or do they mainly happen when you are feeling detached and like things are unreal?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure if they are connected. The thinking trouble seems to happen mostly when I'm sitting at my desk trying to do my work, while that detached feeling usually hits me when I'm riding in a car or near the intersection. I haven't really tracked if they occur at the exact same time.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you noticed any other cognitive issues, such as forgetting recent conversations, misplacing items, or having trouble remembering appointments?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed misplacing things or forgetting conversations, but my ability to manage my schedule and appointments is severely impaired. I've missed multiple scheduled calls, and my wife has had to take over organizing my appointments and paying bills because I just can't think clearly enough to keep track of it all.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of being detached or like things are unreal happen at other times, such as when you are at home or work, or does it only happen when you are in a car or near the accident scene?\n\nPatient: It really only happens in those specific moments, like when I'm riding in a car or walking past the intersection where the crash happened. Everything suddenly feels weirdly far away and fake, like I'm watching a movie of my own life. It feels like I'm not even inside my own body, but just sort of floating a few feet behind myself.\n\nDoctor: Before this accident occurred two weeks ago, had you ever experienced similar episodes of feeling detached from yourself, or had any of these difficulties with concentration and planning?\n\nPatient: No, I have never experienced anything like this before in my life. Before the wreck two weeks ago, I was highly functional, ran my own tax business, and managed my daily responsibilities with absolutely no issues. All of these concentration problems and that detached feeling started strictly after the accident.\n\nDoctor: Did the doctors at the emergency room perform any head imaging, like a CT scan, and have you noticed any sensitivity to light or loud noises since the accident?\n\nPatient: I don't think they did a CT scan at the ER, since they just checked me over pretty quickly and discharged me with minor injuries. I haven't really noticed any sensitivity to light, and while loud noises don't necessarily hurt my ears, sudden noises do make me jump right out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: Even though you haven't had full flashbacks, do you find yourself having sudden, unwanted memories or images of the accident popping into your mind during the day?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience those sudden, unwanted images of the accident popping into my head during the day. Aside from that gap in my memory, I don't have pictures or memories of the wreck randomly showing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you know if you actually lost consciousness during that gap in your memory, and if so, for about how long?\n\nPatient: I don't think I actually lost consciousness, at least according to what the paramedics and hospital staff told me later, but I honestly don't know for sure because of that massive blank spot. There is just a complete, frustrating gap in my mind between seeing those headlights coming at me and suddenly being outside on the stretcher.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the moments when you are in a car, how has your general mood been since the accident? Have you been feeling particularly anxious, down, or hopeless?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I feel sad, but my mood is completely flat and disconnected, to the point where I can't even feel happy or grateful when my wife or son shares good news. At the same time, I feel constantly anxious and on edge, always scanning my surroundings for danger even when I'm just sitting in my own living room. It's like I'm always waiting for something bad to happen again.\n\nDoctor: When you are in a car or near the intersection where the accident happened, do you experience physical reactions like a racing heart, sweating, or difficulty breathing?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience those specific physical reactions like a racing heart, sweating, or trouble breathing when I'm in those situations. For me, it is really just that weird, detached feeling where everything around me suddenly feels fake.\n\nDoctor: Apart from the gap during the accident itself, have you noticed any other gaps in your memory, such as forgetting important personal information or other events that happened before or after that day?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't forgotten any personal information or other events before or after that day. The memory gap is strictly limited to those few minutes of the accident itself, between seeing the headlights and being on the stretcher.\n\nDoctor: Since the accident, have you noticed any new, strong negative thoughts about yourself or the world, such as feeling like you can't trust anyone or that nowhere is safe?\n\nPatient: I haven't really had those specific thoughts about not trusting people or the world being completely unsafe. It's more that I just feel constantly on edge and worried that something bad is about to happen again.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned feeling detached when riding in a car. Have you actually driven a car yourself since the accident, or have you been avoiding getting behind the wheel?\n\nPatient: I haven't actually tried to drive myself since the accident happened. My wife has been driving me everywhere because I just haven't felt up to getting behind the wheel.\n\nDoctor: When you think about driving or passing that intersection, do you actively avoid doing so because you want to prevent that detached feeling or anxiety from happening?\n\nPatient: I haven't really thought about actively trying to avoid those situations. It's just that when I do find myself in a car or near that intersection, that strange, detached feeling always hits me.\n\nDoctor: Even if they aren't scary nightmares, have you had any dreams about the accident, or do you find yourself repeatedly playing the event over in your mind?"

Subtypes: Auditory Hallucinations, Visual Hallucinations, Tactile Hallucinations, Olfactory Hallucinations, Gustatory Hallucinations\n[S021] Disorganized_Thinking_Speech: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.  Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment. Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation. Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.
